There’s no indication that HomeZada has offices or localized services in the UK or Europe. As of now, it appears **US-only** in terms of active marketing and support. (For example, the currency, home financial calculations, and vendor content are U.S. oriented.) European homeowners would find features like the three-year home value forecast and weather alerts less relevant outside the U.S. market. In summary, **HomeZada’s reach is primarily nationwide across the United States**, with little to no formal presence in Europe or the UK.  \n  * **HomeBinder: U.S. nationwide focus.** HomeBinder was founded in the U.S. (Boston area) and has gained traction across all 50 states via home inspectors and real estate partners. They highlight “trusted by more than 950,000 homeowners nationwide” (referring to the United States). The partner network (realtors, lenders, etc.) is U.S.-centric, and many are tied to U.S. home transactions. HomeBinder does not publicly mention any operations in the UK or Europe. Its model of using home inspection firms and MLS-integrated agents wouldn’t directly translate to other countries without significant adaptation. Additionally, things like appliance recall databases and integrations (like Repair Pricer for inspection reports) are U.S.-specific. It’s possible some Canadian professionals use it (given similarity of the North American market), but there’s no explicit note of that. In the UK/EU, there are separate “property logbook” initiatives (see below) filling a similar need, implying HomeBinder itself hasn’t expanded there. So, **HomeBinder is essentially US-only** in its current reach. UK or European customers would need to rely on local solutions, as HomeBinder has not established an overseas presence.  \n* Competitive Strengths & Weaknesses\n\n   **HomeTruth:**\n\n  * *Strengths:* Assuming HomeTruth’s differentiators, its **AI-driven approach** could be a key strength. By leveraging AI and behavioral data, it might offer smarter, proactive recommendations (for saving energy, scheduling maintenance based on use patterns, or flagging cost efficiencies) that competitors don’t have. If it indeed has a **vendor marketplace**, that would be a strength as well – providing a one-stop-shop to not only tell a homeowner *what* needs doing, but also help them *get it done* by booking service pros. Being (presumably) UK-based, HomeTruth could tailor its platform to local needs (integrating UK property data, council regulations, or EU sustainability metrics) – a local focus can be a strength against largely US-only rivals. It might also differentiate on **targeting financial institutions**; for example, if banks or insurance companies back it, HomeTruth could embed into those services and gain trust and distribution.  \n  * *Weaknesses:* As a newer entrant, HomeTruth likely faces the challenge of establishing credibility and user base. It may have **gaps in features** until it matures – for instance, it’s unclear if it matches HomeZada’s breadth (does it have full finance tracking, remodeling budget tools, etc., or is it initially narrower?). If it’s UK-only for now, its addressable market is smaller than a global approach. Also, if HomeTruth relies on AI heavily, it must ensure the AI’s advice is accurate and trustworthy – any mistakes could undermine user confidence. In comparison to HomeBinder’s years in market and HomeZada’s extensive development, HomeTruth might initially lack the **depth of content or integrations** those have (like years of maintenance checklists, or partnerships with insurance). Finally, if its business model is not yet proven, there could be a question of **sustainability** until it gains enough paying users or partners. In summary, HomeTruth’s innovative angle (AI and insights) is its promise, but it must catch up on the foundational features and user trust that the incumbents have.  \n* **HomeZada:**\n\n  * *Strengths:* HomeZada’s biggest strength is its **all-in-one comprehensiveness**. It covers every aspect of home management – from tracking the home’s inventory for insurance, to reminding you of maintenance, to budgeting a renovation, to monitoring home value and equity. This breadth means a user doesn’t need multiple apps; HomeZada consolidates it. Another strength is its **early adoption of AI**: the Zada Homeowner AI provides a forward-looking edge, using image recognition and a chat assistant to simplify data entry and provide expert advice. HomeZada’s long presence (over a decade) gave it time to refine content like maintenance libraries and project templates. It’s also user-friendly enough for individual homeowners while offering **flexibility for professionals** (like realtors can use it too), which broadens its appeal. The platform’s focus on **financial impact** (home value forecasts, ROI of projects) caters to homeowners’ wealth mindset – that’s a unique differentiator versus others that treat home management purely as record-keeping.  \n  * *Weaknesses:* One challenge for HomeZada is that being direct-to-consumer means it must continuously convince individual homeowners to pay for a subscription. Many homeowners are not accustomed to paying for a “home management app,” so **adoption can be slow**. By trying to do so much, HomeZada may feel **complex or overwhelming** to some users who just wanted a simple tool for one task (e.g., a basic home inventory). In contrast, a focused solution might be easier to market. Another weakness is its **limited market localization** – a homeowner in the UK or Europe finds features like US tax tracking or US weather alerts irrelevant, and HomeZada hasn’t addressed those markets, leaving room for local competitors. Also, while HomeZada has many features, it may have **gaps in execution**; for example, it relies on the user to input a lot of data (even with AI help, not everything can be automated), so keeping the information up-to-date requires user diligence (some might lapse in usage over time). Lastly, HomeZada doesn’t have an integrated service marketplace, which means after it tells a user what to fix, the user must still go find contractors elsewhere – a less “actionable” outcome compared to platforms that facilitate hiring help. This could be seen as a limitation in convenience.  \n* **HomeBinder:**\n\n  * *Strengths:* HomeBinder’s **distribution model** is a major strength – by partnering with home inspectors, realtors, and lenders, it gets introduced to homeowners at the perfect moment (when they’ve just bought a home and have tons of info to organize). This gives it a steady funnel of users without heavy consumer marketing. Its focus on being a **secure, long-term data repository** resonates with homeowners who want a “household diary” that stays with the house. It has a very tangible use case during resale: a well-maintained HomeBinder can be passed to a buyer to increase confidence in the home’s history. HomeBinder also emphasizes **privacy and no spam**, which is appealing to users wary of their data being sold (this stance sets it apart from some tech platforms). Features like appliance recall alerts and the new utility setup concierge add to its value proposition at key points of ownership. Furthermore, HomeBinder’s integration of **professional input** (e.g., an inspector pre-populating the binder) means the data can be very accurate and detailed without the homeowner doing all the initial work.  \n  * *Weaknesses:* HomeBinder’s reliance on B2B partners can also be a double-edged sword. It means that if you didn’t receive one when you bought your home, you might not easily get access later – **organic consumer sign-up is not straightforward** (it’s not openly advertised for individual purchase). This limits its reach to only those who go through certain channels. In terms of features, HomeBinder is strong on documentation and reminders, but it lacks some of the **analytics or “smart” insights** that AI-driven competitors are offering. There’s no AI chat to ask questions, no predictive financial modeling – it’s more reactive in nature. Some users may find the interface or experience defined by the needs of its professional partners rather than the homeowner (for instance, it might carry the branding of the realtor or have extra info useful to an inspector). Also, while it has a vendor list, HomeBinder by policy avoids unsolicited offers, so it doesn’t actively connect homeowners to new service providers unless the homeowner seeks it – a conservative approach that could be seen as a gap if the user actually wants help finding contractors. Internationally, it’s essentially nonexistent, so any strengths are confined to the U.S. market. Lastly, **monetization through partners** might limit how fast HomeBinder can evolve features; they need to keep partners happy (who might be primarily concerned with branding and client retention) which could slow down consumer-facing innovation compared to a purely consumer-driven product.\n\n### **UK & European Competitors in AI-Driven Property Management**\n\n* In the UK and Europe, several PropTech companies offer features similar to or competing with HomeTruth/HomeZada/HomeBinder. These range from digital property logbooks for homeowners to AI-powered tools for property maintenance. Key players and their positioning include:\n\n  * **OurProperty (UK):** A popular **digital property logbook platform** for UK homes. It allows any homeowner (or even renters and agents) to “claim” a digital logbook for their property and store all details in one place. OurProperty integrates extensive UK property data – it has data on 29 million properties and offers alerts on things like nearby planning applications or when a house is listed for sale. Homeowners can add maintenance records, utility info, and share the logbook with others easily. Its business model includes selling **personalized “Property Plates”** (a kind of domain-name for your house) and possibly premium features; it claims over 256,000 users have claimed logbooks, showing strong traction in the UK. OurProperty’s positioning is very homeowner-centric, focusing on **making home information accessible and even public-facing** (if owners choose to share data). It directly competes as a digital home management record, though it emphasizes property data and monitoring over AI-driven insights.\n\n     [ourproperty.co.uk](https://www.ourproperty.co.uk/#:~:text=Create%20your%20own%20Property%20Logbook)\n\n  * **Chimni (UK):** A leading example of the **digital property logbook** concept in the UK. Chimni provides a “digital companion for the life of a property” – a secure online logbook where all property information, documents, and history can be stored. It’s free for homeowners to use, and one of its aims is to speed up and add transparency to property transactions (a Chimni logbook can be shared with buyers to show a home’s full history). Chimni integrates with government and supplier websites, meaning it can pull in data like planning permissions or energy data automatically. It’s involved with UK industry initiatives; for example, it helped establish consumer data standards for new homes and is a member of the Residential Logbook Association (which standardizes these services). Chimni is positioned as a **homeowner-facing tool** that’s also useful for professionals like conveyancers and retrofit specialists. While not explicitly “AI-driven” in a chatbot sense, it does deal in smart integration of data sources. It competes in making a **holistic home record** much like HomeBinder or HomeZada’s document module, but very tailored to UK needs (compliance, conveyancing, etc.). Its strength is being a **neutral platform for data** (with an eye on future regulations requiring more digital info at sale time).\n\n     [chimni.net](https://www.chimni.net/#:~:text=A%20Chimni%20logbook%20is%20a,up%20to%20control%20its)\n\n     [chimni.com](https://www.chimni.com/#:~:text=Chimni%20,and%20security%20when%20you%20sell)\n\n     [chimni.com](https://chimni.com/why-have-a-chimni-log/#:~:text=Why%20Have%20a%20Chimni%20Logbook,supplier%20web%20sites%20and%20accounts)\n\n     [todaysconveyancer.co.uk](https://todaysconveyancer.co.uk/logbook-company-establishes-first-ever-consumer-data-standard-new-homes/#:~:text=Logbook%20company%20establishes%20first,the%20data%20and%20digital%20information)\n\n     [chimni.net](https://www.chimni.net/about#:~:text=About%20Chimni%20Chimni%20is%20the,Build%20properties%20and%20Retrofit%20specialists)\n\n     [chimni.net](https://www.chimni.net/about#:~:text=About%20Chimni%20Chimni%20is%20the,Build%20properties%20and%20Retrofit%20specialists)\n\n  * **Plentific (UK/Germany):** An **AI-enabled property maintenance and management platform** initially from the UK. Plentific primarily serves landlords, property managers, and housing associations (B2B), but it addresses a similar space of managing property upkeep. It offers an end-to-end solution that allows property owners/managers to **find and hire vetted contractors** through its marketplace of over 18,000 tradespeople. Plentific streamlines repair requests, scheduling, and payments, bringing **transparency and efficiency** to maintenance. It generates analytics on property operations and ensures compliance on tasks, some of which involve AI (e.g., routing tasks to the best contractor, or predicting maintenance needs). Plentific’s revenue model includes subscription fees from enterprise clients and transaction fees on jobs. It has expanded beyond the UK into **Europe and even the U.S.** in the multifamily sector. While a homeowner with a single house wouldn’t use Plentific directly, it’s a competitor in the broader **PropTech ecosystem** because it excels at **vendor management and maintenance workflows**, something HomeTruth or HomeZada aim to provide on a smaller scale. Plentific’s positioning: a powerful **operations platform with a marketplace**, helping property owners (especially large portfolios) keep their properties in top shape while saving time.\n\n     [canvasbusinessmodel.com](https://canvasbusinessmodel.com/blogs/how-it-works/plentific-how-it-works?srsltid=AfmBOoqdYz0c_Y5XyNopbna16d1bhiJN1KbkFanWvAsGP94rtLAVB524#:~:text=One%20of%20the%20key%20features,maintained%20and%20in%20good%20condition)\n\n     [canvasbusinessmodel.com](https://canvasbusinessmodel.com/blogs/how-it-works/plentific-how-it-works?srsltid=AfmBOoqdYz0c_Y5XyNopbna16d1bhiJN1KbkFanWvAsGP94rtLAVB524#:~:text=,informed%20decisions%20about%20their%20properties)\n\n  * **AskPorter (UK):** An **AI-driven communications and management platform** for property management. AskPorter uses a chatbot called “Porter” to automate interactions between tenants, landlords, property managers, and service providers. It is essentially a 24/7 digital property assistant that can handle queries (like a tenant reporting an issue), schedule repairs, and manage tasks through a no-code workflow system. This platform leverages AI to reduce the manual workload on property managers by triaging maintenance issues and even **initiating routine tasks automatically**. While its focus is more on rental property management, the underlying idea of an AI assistant overlaps with the AI features of HomeZada or HomeTruth. AskPorter has been used in social housing contexts as well, demonstrating its ability to handle scale and repetitive processes. It’s positioned as a tool to **increase efficiency and responsiveness in property management**. As a competitor, AskPorter highlights the power of AI in reducing the friction of property upkeep. A homeowner using HomeTruth might not use AskPorter, but a property manager might choose AskPorter over building their own AI assistant – thus it’s a notable AI PropTech player in Europe. Its strength is clearly the **AI chatbot for property issues**, a niche that none of HomeZada/HomeBinder directly cover.\n\n     [builtworld.com](https://www.builtworld.com/company/askporter#:~:text=askporter%20,workflow%20system%20eliminate%20mundane)\n\n     [askporter.com](https://www.askporter.com/#:~:text=The%20AI%20assistant%20for%20real,code%20platform)\n\n     [builtworld.com](https://www.builtworld.com/company/askporter#:~:text=askporter%20,workflow%20system%20eliminate%20mundane)\n\n     [askporter.com](https://www.askporter.com/#:~:text=The%20AI%20assistant%20for%20real,code%20platform)\n\n     [askporter.com](https://www.askporter.com/social-housing#:~:text=Social%20Housing%20%7C%20askporter%20,This)\n\n  * **Property Logbook Initiatives (UK/EU):** Beyond specific companies, it’s worth noting that the **concept of digital property logbooks** is gaining government and industry support in the UK and Europe. Organizations and consortiums are pushing for standardized home information packs that stay with a property. For example, the UK’s Department for Levelling Up, Housing & Communities has shown interest in logbooks, and the Home Buying and Selling Group in the UK encourages their use. Companies like [**PropertyLogbook.co.uk**](http://PropertyLogbook.co.uk) **(by Etive)** and others under the Residential Logbook Association umbrella compete in this space. Their positioning is often tied to real estate transactions – ensuring all maintenance, warranties, permits, and modifications are documented to facilitate easier sales and compliance. While not “AI” per se, some are exploring AI to populate or verify data. These initiatives indicate that **European competitors often angle towards compliance, energy efficiency tracking, and facilitating sales**, complementing the home management features. A platform like HomeTruth entering the UK market faces competition from these logbook services that are becoming more common, sometimes backed by authorities or large industry players.\n\n     [designingbuildings.co.uk](https://www.designingbuildings.co.uk/wiki/Property%20Logbooks#:~:text=,a%20Digital%20Property%20Logbook)\n\n     [designingbuildings.co.uk](https://www.designingbuildings.co.uk/wiki/Property%20Logbooks#:~:text=,Purpose%20of%20A%20Property%20Logbook)\n\n     [designingbuildings.co.uk](https://www.designingbuildings.co.uk/wiki/Property%20Logbooks#:~:text=,Logbooks%20Around%20The%20World)\n\n     [homebuyingsellingcouncil.co.uk](https://homebuyingsellingcouncil.co.uk/property-logbooks-workgroup/#:~:text=Property%20Logbooks%20,form%2C%20by%20vendors%20and%20buyers)\n\n     [propertylogbook.co.uk](https://www.propertylogbook.co.uk/#:~:text=,presentation%20of%20material%20information%20upfront)\n\n     [propertylogbook.co.uk](https://www.propertylogbook.co.uk/#:~:text=,this%20as%20and%20when%20required)\n\n  * **Other Notable Players:** In continental Europe, PropTech for home management is a bit fragmented but growing. For instance, **Infogrid (UK)** provides AI-driven building monitoring (though more commercial buildings), and **Switchee (UK)** uses smart thermostats and AI analytics to help landlords in social housing detect issues like fuel poverty or maintenance needs proactively. **Nines (formerly Housepad, US but serving UK/EU)** offers a high-end household management app – essentially a digital house manual for affluent homes with staff, focusing on tasks, maintenance, and service provider coordination. Additionally, **Homebot (US)** deserves mention even though US-based: it’s an AI-infused home finance dashboard offered via lenders that keeps homeowners engaged with their home equity and suggests when to refinance or sell. Homebot’s success in the U.S. (millions of users via banks) signals a model European banks might adopt to stay in touch with mortgage clients, potentially bringing competition in the form of **home valuation and finance insight tools**.\n\n     [tracxn.com](https://tracxn.com/d/artificial-intelligence/ai-startups-in-property-management-tech-in-united-kingdom/__B7qZL6HfGPfsHVjuBrniEb51xXxay3I-tKCfkmq1FGQ/companies#:~:text=Artificial%20Intelligence%20,which%20include%20Infogrid%2C%20Verv%2C%20Measurable)\n\n     [ninesliving.com](https://ninesliving.com/household-management-app-family-organizing/#:~:text=Household%20Management%20App%20,from%20an%20estate%20management%20expert)\n\n* In summary, the UK and European landscape has a strong emphasis on **digital logbooks and maintenance marketplaces**. Companies like OurProperty and Chimni compete on creating a comprehensive home record similar to HomeBinder’s concept, while Plentific and AskPorter leverage AI and marketplaces to handle the maintenance and management side, akin to the service coordination features that HomeTruth or HomeZada might offer. Each of these competitors has carved out a particular niche – whether it’s transactional facilitation, tenant communication, or repair fulfillment – but all are converging on the idea that technology (especially AI and centralized data) can vastly improve how properties are managed and how homeowners/landlords make decisions about their real estate assets.\n\n   **Sources:**\n\n  * HomeBinder overview and features; about HomeBinder and partner-only model.\n\n  * HomeZada features and AI capabilities; HomeZada pricing plans.\n\n     [blog.homezada.com](http://blog.homezada.com)\n\n  * HomeZada target audiences (homeowners vs professionals).\n\n     [homezada.com](https://www.homezada.com/homeowners/homeowner-ai#:~:text=Professionals)\n\n  * HomeBinder user base in U.S..\n\n  * [canvasbusinessmodel.com](https://canvasbusinessmodel.com/blogs/how-it-works/plentific-how-it-works?srsltid=AfmBOoqdYz0c_Y5XyNopbna16d1bhiJN1KbkFanWvAsGP94rtLAVB524#:~:text=,informed%20decisions%20about%20their%20properties)\n\n Strategy**\n\n## **Latest Business Model**\n\nBusiness\\_Model\\_2025-02-14\\_14-02-16.xlsx\n\n### **But…..**\n\nFocusing solely on CPL from digital channels may be too limited. Incorporating additional metrics—like organic conversion rates, referral growth, and partner-driven leads—will give you a fuller picture of your market potential.\n\nThese additional channels can dramatically increase subscriber numbers without proportionally increasing costs, resulting in a much more favorable CAC and LTV ratio than a basic CPL-driven model would indicate.\n\nYou can approach subscriber calibration from several additional angles:\n\n1. **Funnel & Conversion Metrics:**  \n   * Instead of starting with spend, work from expected website traffic and engagement data. Estimate the percentage of visitors who become MQLs, then SQLs, and finally paying subscribers. This top–down funnel approach helps validate the bottom–up numbers.  \n2. **Market Penetration & TAM/SAM:**  \n   * Use your Total Addressable Market (TAM) and Serviceable Available Market (SAM) figures to estimate what realistic market penetration looks like over time. For instance, if you target a specific segment of tech-savvy homeowners and you assume a 1–2% annual penetration rate, that provides another subscriber projection.  \n3. **Competitor Benchmarks:**  \n   * Analyze similar products in the PropTech space (like HomeZada or HomeBinder, where available) to see what conversion rates or subscriber numbers they’ve achieved relative to their user base. This benchmarking can give you an external calibration.  \n4. **Pilot Studies & User Surveys:**  \n   * Conduct small-scale pilots or user surveys to gauge real-world interest. Measure engagement, willingness to pay, and actual conversion rates, then scale those numbers based on your projected market size.  \n5. **Viral & Referral Effects:**  \n   * Factor in potential network effects. If your product has high utility and you implement referral incentives, you might see organic subscriber growth that isn’t directly tied to marketing spend. Model different scenarios based on referral-driven growth.  \n6. **Retention & Upsell Metrics:**  \n   * Finally, consider not just acquisition but also retention. A product that delivers strong value may see higher lifetime engagement and upsell to higher tiers, indirectly boosting your subscriber base over time.\n\n---\n\n# **Web Traffic of UK Real Estate Platforms by Audience Segment**\n\n* First-Time Homebuyer Online Traffic\n\n   First-time buyers in the UK overwhelmingly turn to digital platforms – both property portals and mortgage tools – when starting their homeownership journey. Key online traffic indicators include:\n\n  * **Property Portals (First-Time Buyer Searches):** Major listing sites attract huge audiences. Rightmove, the UK’s largest property portal, sees about **127–140 million visits per month**. This equates to roughly **2 visits per month per UK resident**, underscoring how common property browsing is. Zoopla, the second-largest portal, draws around **50–60 million monthly visits** across its websites and apps (including PrimeLocation). Many of these visitors are first-time buyers researching homes and prices. Notably, the **25–34 age group** (prime first-time buyer age) is the largest segment of Rightmove’s audience, suggesting a significant share of traffic comes from younger buyers.\n\n     [propertyroad.co.uk](https://www.propertyroad.co.uk/15-reasons-house-not-selling-how-avoid-them/#:~:text=1,50%20million%20visits%20per%20month)\n\n     [hub.rightmove.co.uk](https://hub.rightmove.co.uk/content/uploads/2022/02/Rightmove-valuation-pack-leaflet-lettings-2022.pdf?utm_source=&utm_medium=&utm_campaign=BPG&brandID=&utm_content=#:~:text=Rightmove%20gets%20over%20140%20million,visits%20per%20month)\n\n     [propertyroad.co.uk](https://www.propertyroad.co.uk/15-reasons-house-not-selling-how-avoid-them/#:~:text=month,50%20million%20visits%20per%20month)\n\n  * **Mortgage Comparison Tools:** First-time buyers also rely on online services to explore mortgage options. For example, **MoneySuperMarket**, a popular UK price comparison site, reports over **13 million users each month** interacting with its services – a portion of which is driven by mortgage rate comparisons and first-time buyer searches. Other fintech and PropTech services (like digital mortgage brokers) also see heavy usage; Habito, for instance, has served over 550,000 customers since launch, indicating strong demand for online mortgage assistance. This high web engagement reflects first-time buyers’ tendency to **“shop around” online for both properties and financing** before committing.\n\n     [mrjrecruitment.com](https://www.mrjrecruitment.com/case-studies/case-study/moneysupermarket/#:~:text=MoneySuperMarket%20)\n\n     [ftadviser.com](https://www.ftadviser.com/mortgages/2023/10/19/habito-hits-profitability-in-september-for-first-time/#:~:text=FTAdviser%20www,550%2C000%20since%20launching%20in%202016)\n\n* *Per capita, the combined traffic to real estate portals and mortgage sites is substantial.* For perspective, **Rightmove \\+ Zoopla together approach \\~180+ million visits in a typical month**, which is roughly **2.7–3 site visits per UK inhabitant** on average. In practice, of course, active home-seekers (especially first-timers) account for the bulk of those visits – often visiting these sites daily or weekly during their search. The data shows that **online platforms are an essential first-stop** for first-time buyers in the UK property market.\n\n* Overall Homebuyer Web Traffic in the UK\n\n   Beyond just first-timers, overall consumer interest in property translates into **hundreds of millions of website visits** to real estate platforms. The major UK property websites dominate this space and see high engagement from the general homebuying public:\n\n  * **Rightmove – Market Leader:** Rightmove is by far the most visited real estate website in the UK, averaging **over 140 million visits per month** (per 2021 analytics). For context, Rightmove’s **monthly traffic** alone is nearly twice that of its nearest competitor’s entire network. During peak market periods, these numbers climb even higher – **Rightmove recorded 218 million visits in January 2022**, its busiest month on record. This portal’s audience size means **about 80% of all time that Brits spend on property portals is on Rightmove**. In 2022, users spent an **average of 1.35 billion minutes per month** on Rightmove’s site and app, reflecting deeply engaged browsing (with **sessions averaging \\~10–11 minutes** long as users scroll through listings).\n\n     [hub.rightmove.co.uk](https://hub.rightmove.co.uk/content/uploads/2022/02/Rightmove-valuation-pack-leaflet-lettings-2022.pdf?utm_source=&utm_medium=&utm_campaign=BPG&brandID=&utm_content=#:~:text=Rightmove%20gets%20over%20140%20million,visits%20per%20month)\n\n     [thenegotiator.co.uk](https://thenegotiator.co.uk/news/property-portals-traffic-uk-zpg/#:~:text=These%20latest%20ZPG%20figures%20would,arts%20of%20Google%20traffic%20manipulation)\n\n     [mpamag.com](https://www.mpamag.com/uk/mortgage-types/residential/rightmove-busiest-ever-january-recorded-in-2022/391258#:~:text=year%2C%2053,compared%20to%202019)\n\n     [rightmove.co.uk](https://www.rightmove.co.uk/c/claims/#:~:text=More%20than%2080,the%20UK%20is%20on%20Rightmove)\n\n     [onlinemarketplaces.com](https://www.onlinemarketplaces.com/articles/rightmove-fy22-financials-strong-as-ever/#:~:text=,%E2%80%9D)\n\n     [semrush.com](https://www.semrush.com/website/rightmove.co.uk/overview/#:~:text=Benchmark%20website%E2%80%99s%20performance%20against%20your,57)\n\n  * **Zoopla and Other Major Portals:** Zoopla is the second-largest property website, attracting roughly **50–60 million visits each month**. (Zoopla’s figure includes its affiliated sites like PrimeLocation.) This is about **0.7 visits per UK person per month**, showing a broad reach albeit smaller than Rightmove’s. The third player, **OnTheMarket**, has a more modest share; it was getting around **14 million monthly visits** in 2018 and has grown since (reporting \\~138 million total visits in one recent 6-month period, equivalent to \\~23 million per month in 2022). Together, **these portals see on the order of \\~200 million+ visits per month**, or roughly **3+ visits per capita**, when the market is active. This massive traffic volume underscores Brits’ keen interest in property – whether actively house-hunting, checking prices, or just browsing dream homes.\n\n     [propertyroad.co.uk](https://www.propertyroad.co.uk/15-reasons-house-not-selling-how-avoid-them/#:~:text=month,50%20million%20visits%20per%20month)\n\n     [thenegotiator.co.uk](https://thenegotiator.co.uk/news/property-portals-traffic-uk-zpg/#:~:text=,ahead%20of%20OnTheMarket%E2%80%99s%2014%20million)\n\n  * **User Intent & Engagement:** Importantly, a large portion of this web traffic comes from serious homebuyers (and renters) actively planning moves, not just casual viewers. According to a Rightmove survey, **83% of its users are considering a move**, and over half (51%) **intend to move within the next 12 months**. This indicates that the portals’ audiences are highly intent-driven. Users aren’t just glancing at listings; many are actively comparing areas, tracking prices, and contacting agents. (In fact, **time-on-site metrics** reinforce this: collectively, visitors spend **billions of minutes** on property sites annually.) All of this points to strong engagement: the typical home seeker will return to these websites repeatedly throughout their decision process – evidenced by Rightmove’s slogan of being the place people “turn **and return** to” for property searches.\n\n     [hub.rightmove.co.uk](https://hub.rightmove.co.uk/69-of-buyers-need-to-sell-in-order-to-buy/#:~:text=The%20vast%20majority%20of%20Rightmove%E2%80%99s,audience%20is%20open%20to%20moving)\n\n     [onlinemarketplaces.com](https://www.onlinemarketplaces.com/articles/rightmove-fy22-financials-strong-as-ever/#:~:text=Web%20traffic%20was%2034,minutes%20spent%20on%20the%20platform)\n\n     [rightmove.co.uk](https://www.rightmove.co.uk/c/claims/#:~:text=Rightmove%20is%20the%20UK%E2%80%99s%20leading,market%20trends%2C%20maps%20and%20schools)\n\n* In summary, **overall homebuyer interest in the UK translates to very high web traffic on major real estate platforms**. The combination of large user bases and deep engagement means these sites function as a crucial hub for the UK housing market, effectively becoming a barometer of homebuyer demand online.\n\n* Landlords and Second-Home Owners: Digital Engagement\n\n   **Landlords and second-home owners** represent another important segment of the UK property market, and they too have embraced PropTech and online platforms. While smaller in number than regular homebuyers, their online activity is significant in specific areas:\n\n  * **Market Size – Landlords & Second Homes:** The UK has a substantial population of property owners beyond primary residences. Recent data shows there are roughly **2.8 million private landlords** in the UK (tax-year 2021/22). In addition, about **772,000 households in England (3% of households) report owning a second home** (as of 2018-19) – this includes holiday homes and investment properties. These figures highlight a large cohort of individuals who may be *renting out properties* or *holding second residences*. Their needs (finding tenants, managing properties, or buying additional homes) drive them to specific online services.\n\n     [property118.com](https://www.property118.com/number-of-uks-unincorporated-landlords-rises-to-2-82-million/#:~:text=The%20number%20of%20unincorporated%20landlords,HMRC)\n\n     [assets.publishing.service.gov.uk](https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5f044501e90e075c50609e43/2020_EHS_second_homes_factsheet.pdf#:~:text=In%202018,Most)\n\n  * **Online Platforms for Landlords:** Landlords are highly active online, especially for advertising rentals and managing investments. Many rely on the same big property portals via letting agents – effectively tapping into the huge renter audience on those sites. For example, an industry survey found **3 in 4 renters find their home on Rightmove**. This means landlords (or their agents) almost universally **list rental properties on portals like Rightmove/Zoopla**, leveraging the portals’ traffic to secure tenants. Alongside portals, dedicated landlord services have grown rapidly. **OpenRent**, a popular online letting platform that connects landlords directly with tenants, claims over **5 million registered users as of 2022**. It has facilitated more than 1.3 million tenant placements to date, illustrating the **strong digital uptake among landlords** for self-service rental tools. Additionally, landlords frequently use comparison sites to source **buy-to-let mortgages and insurance** – similar to residential buyers – contributing to traffic on financial platforms. (There are over **2 million buy-to-let mortgages outstanding in the UK**, and many landlords turn to web-based comparison and broker sites to refinance or expand their portfolio.)\n\n     [rightmove.co.uk](https://www.rightmove.co.uk/c/claims/#:~:text=Only%2C%20all%20aged%2015%2B%20using,s)\n\n     [hybr.co.uk](https://www.hybr.co.uk/landlords/blog/openrent-dumps-rightmove#:~:text=If%20you%20have%20been%20living,first%20ever%20online%20letting%20agents)\n\n     [ukfinance.org.uk](https://www.ukfinance.org.uk/news-and-insight/press-release/uk-finance-mortgage-data#:~:text=Split%20of%20fixed%20vs%20variable%E2%80%8B%E2%80%8B,also%20being%20on%20fixed%20rates)\n\n  * **Second-Home Owners & Investors:** Those owning second homes or investing in property also exhibit notable online behavior. When **searching for second properties or investment buys**, they contribute to traffic on mainstream property portals – often using filters for buy-to-let opportunities or holiday locations. Once they’ve acquired a property, many second-home owners go online to monetize or manage these assets. A significant number use **short-term rental platforms** (a form of PropTech) to rent out holiday homes. For instance, Airbnb and similar sites are popular with second-home owners in tourist areas. However, the scale of this segment, while growing, is still limited relative to the overall housing stock – an analysis found that at any given time **fewer than 0.7% of UK dwellings are listed as entire-home rentals on Airbnb**. This suggests that only a fraction of second-home owners engage in short-term letting, but those who do generate substantial digital activity (Airbnb itself sees tens of millions of site visits monthly globally). Furthermore, property investors often engage with online forums, landlord portals, and market data websites (such as LandlordZone, Property118, or auction sites) to inform their decisions, though traffic for these niche sites is smaller and not always publicly reported.\n\n     [news.airbnb.com](https://news.airbnb.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/4/2024/09/EY_Airbnb_Housing-and-Economic-Impacts-in-the-UK.pdf#:~:text=,as%20entire%20homes%20to%20let)\n\n* *Engagement metrics for landlords and second-home owners tend to be reflected in specific online outcomes:* for example, the **volume of rental listings** and leads on portals, or the **growth of online landlord communities and tools**. A testament to their digital engagement is that **80% of UK rental leads now originate from online portals** (with Rightmove alone accounting for 75% of lettings leads)\n\n   [rightmove.co.uk](https://www.rightmove.co.uk/c/claims/#:~:text=More%20homes%20are%20successfully%20sold,Rightmove%20than%20any%20other%20portal)\n\n   . This indicates that landlords almost universally use online channels to reach tenants. Likewise, the ease of managing second properties through apps and platforms means higher adoption of PropTech solutions in this group year over year.\n\n   **In summary, UK landlords and second-home owners are highly digitally active**, albeit in targeted ways. They drive significant traffic to rental listing services and property management platforms. Whether it’s a small landlord using an app to advertise a flat, or a second-home owner listing a cottage on a holiday-let site, this segment’s engagement underscores the broader digital transformation of real estate. With millions of landlords and nearly a half-million domestic second homes, their collective online footprint – from Rightmove listings to niche PropTech services – forms an integral part of the UK property web landscape.\n\n* Sources\n\n  * Rightmove average monthly visits and portal market share\n\n housing stock\n\n
